0.826

We validate our hypothesis by running the
Ping Pong

micro-benchmark with UI requirements — we summarise
the results in
Table

2
.
By requiring additional text to be shown on send
and terminal states,
we observe a noticeable increase
in the message processing time for
bare
,
whereas relatively insignificant changes on
mpst
.
The UI requirements demand
bare

to perform additional state updates and re-rendering,
reducing the overhead relative to
mpst
.

Scalability

As the generated APIs abstract away the details of the actual destination of a
message, the effect of scaling the number of roles in a protocol is
transparent to the developer.
The number of states and transitions in the EFSM increases as the complexity of
the protocol scales.
The generated
React.js
runtime re-renders the UI on every state transition,
so more complex protocols would trigger more re-renders, incurring performance penalties.
However, this is less of a concern for
STScript
, as user-facing application
protocols in interactive web settings tend not to be large in size (cf. distributed algorithms in large scale systems).

6.
Related and Future Work

There are a vast number of
studies on theories of
session types
(
Hüttel et al. 2016
)
, some of which are
integrated in programming languages
(
Ancona
et al. 2016
)
,
or implemented as tools
(
Simon Gay 2017
)
.
Here we focus on the most closely related work:

(1)

code generation from session types;

(2)

web applications based on session types;
and
(3)

encoding multiparty sessions into binary connections.

Code Generation from Session Types

In general, a code generation toolchain takes a protocol (session type)
description (in a domain specific language) and produces
well-typed APIs

conforming to the protocol.
The Scribble
(
Scribble
Authors 2015
;
Yoshida
et al. 2014
)
language is
widely used to describe multiparty protocols, agnostic to target
languages.
The Scribble toolchain implements the projection of global protocols, and
the construction of endpoint finite state machines (EFSM).
Many implementations use an EFSM-based approach to generate APIs
for target programming languages,
e.g. Java
(
Hu and Yoshida 2016
)
,
Go
(
Castro
et al. 2019
)
, and F#
(
Neykova
et al. 2018
)
, for
distributed applications.
Our work also falls into this category, where we generate

correct-by-construction

TypeScript
APIs, but focusing on interactive web
applications.
Following
(
Zhou
et al. 2020
)
, we
generate callback-style APIs, adapted to fit
the event-driven paradigm in web programming.

Alternatively,
Demangeon et al. 2015
propose MPST-based

runtime monitors
to
dynamically
verify protocol conformance, also
available from code generation.
Whilst a runtime approach is viable for JavaScript applications,
our method, which leverages the
TypeScript
type system to
statically

provide communication safety to developers,
gives a more rigorous guarantee.

Ng
et al. 2015
propose a different kind of MPST-based code generation,
where sequential C code can be parallelised according to a global protocol
using MPI.

Session-Typed Web Development

Fowler 2020
integrates
binary
session types into
web application development. Our work encodes
multiparty
session types for web applications,

subsuming
binary sessions.

King
et al. 2019
extend the Scribble toolchain for web applications
targeting PureScript
(
PureScript 2020
)
, a functional web programming language.
In their work, a client may only communicate with one
designated
server
role,
whereas our work addresses this limitation via
routing
through a designated
role. Jolie
(
Montesi 2016
;
The Jolie Team 2020
)
is a programming language designed for
web services, capable of expressing multiparty sessions.
Jolie extends the concept of choreography
programming
(
Carbone and
Montesi 2013
)
, where a choreography contains
behaviour of all participants, and endpoints are derived directly from
projections.
Our work implements each endpoint separately.
Moreover, we generate server and
client endpoints using different styles to better fit their use case.
Note that Links
(
Cooper
et al. 2007
)
, PureScript
(
PureScript 2020
)
and Jolie
(
The Jolie Team 2020
)

are not usually considered mainstream
in modern web programming, whereas our tool targets popular web
programming technologies.

Encoding of Multiparty Session Types

RouST
models an “orchestrating” role (the
router) for forwarding messages between roles, and this information
is used to directly guide

STScript
to correctly implement the protocol
in
Node.js
.
The use of a
medium
process
to encode multiparty into binary session types
has been studied in theoretical settings,
in particular, linear logic based session types
(
Caires and
Pérez 2016
;
Carbone et al. 2016
;
Carbone et al. 2015
)
.
In their setting, one medium process
is used for orchestrating the multiparty communications
between all roles
in binary session types.Our encoding models the nature of web applications
running over WebSockets, where browser clients can only
directly connect to a server, not other clients.

Scalas
et al. 2017
show a different encoding of multiparty session types
into linear
\pi
-calculus, which
decomposes a multiparty session into binary channels

without
a medium process.
This encoding is used to implement MPST with binary session types in Scala.
Their approach uses
session delegation
, i.e. passing channels,
which is difficult to implement with WebSockets.
Our
RouST
focuses on modelling the routing mechanism
at the
global types level
,
so that our encoding can directly guide
correct practical implementations.

Conclusion and Future Work

We explore the application of session types to modern interactive web
programming, using code generation for communication-safe
APIs from multiparty protocol specifications.
We incorporate routing semantics to seamlessly adapt MPST to address the
practical challenges of using WebSocket protocols.
Our approach integrates with popular industrial frameworks, and is
backed by our theory of
RouST
,
guaranteeing communication safety.

For future work, we would like to extend
(1)

STScript
with additional practical
extensions of MPST, e.g. explicit connections
(
Hu and Yoshida 2017
)
,

(2)
our code generation approach to implement
typestates in
TypeScript
, inspired by
(
Kouzapas
et al. 2016
)
.

Acknowledgements.
We thank the CC reviewers for their comments and suggestions.
We thank Neil Sayers for testing the artifact.
The work is supported by EPSRC EP/T006544/1, EP/K011715/1, EP/K034413/1,
EP/L00058X/1, EP/N027833/1, EP/N028201/1, EP/T014709/1 and
EP/V000462/1, and NCSS/EPSRC VeTSS.

Appendix A
Artifact Appendix

A.1.
Abstract

This artifact provides the implementation of the

STScript
toolchain.
We provide a number of web applications implemented using the
APIs generated from
STScript
to test the expressiveness of our approach.
We also provide a set of tools to execute performance benchmarks
to test the performance of
STScript
.

A.2.
Artifact check-list (meta-information)

•

Algorithm:

TypeScript
code generation for
Node.js
and
React.js
endpoints from Scribble protocol specification;
Formalism of routed multiparty session types.

•

Compilation:

Python
3.7+ for using
STScript
;
TypeScript
3.7.4+ for compiling endpoint programs that use the generated APIs.

•

Transformations:

Compilation to
TypeScript
.

•

Binary:

Source code and scripts included to build the Docker image
from the sources.

•

Hardware:

Experiments were carried out using a machine equipped with
Intel i7-4850HQ CPU (2.3 GHz, 4 cores, 8 threads), 16 GB RAM,
macOS operating system version 11.1.

•

Execution:

We include scripts to run tests, build the case studies and
run/visualise the benchmarks.

•

Metrics:

Message processing times.

•

Output:

Benchmark execution times.

•

Experiments:

Case studies of web applications implemented using the generated APIs;
Performance micro-benchmarks.

A.5.
Experiment Workflow

A.5.1.
End-to-End Tests

To run the end-to-end tests:

⬇

#

Run

from

any

directory

$

run_tests

The end-to-end tests verify that

•

STScript
correctly parses the Scribble protocol specification files,
and,

•

STScript
correctly generates
TypeScript
APIs, and,

•

The generated APIs can be type-checked by the
TypeScript
Compiler
successfully.

The protocol specification files, describing the multiparty communication, are
located in
\sim
/codegen/tests/system/examples
.
The generated APIs are saved under
\sim
/web-sandbox
(which is a
sandbox environment set up for the
TypeScript
Compiler) and are deleted when the test
finishes.

Passing the end-to-end tests means that our
STScript
toolchain correctly
generates type-correct
TypeScript
code.

A.5.2.
Case Studies

We include three case studies of realistic web applications, namely
Noughts and
Crosses
,
Travel Agency
and
Battleships
,
implemented using the generated APIs to show the expressiveness
of the generated APIs and the compatibility with modern web
programming practices.

Noughts and Crosses

This is the classic turn-based 2-player game as introduced in

Section

5
.
To generate the APIs for both players and the game server:

⬇

#

Run

from

any

directory

$

build_noughts
-
and
-
crosses

To run the case study:

⬇

$

cd

~/
case
-
studies
/
NoughtsAndCrosses

$

npm

start

Visit
http://localhost:8080
on two web browser windows
side-by-side, one for each player.
Play the game;
you may refer to
https://youtu.be/SBANcdwpYPw
for an example game execution
as a starting point.

You may also verify the following:

(1)

Open 4 web browsers to play 2 games simultaneously.
Observe that the state of each game board is consistent with its
game, i.e. moves do not get propagated to the wrong game.

(2)

Open 2 web browsers to play a game, and close one of them
mid-game. Observe that the remaining web browser is notified that
their opponent has forfeited the match.

Additional Notes

Refresh both web browsers to start a new game.

Stop the web application by pressing
Ctrl+C
on the terminal.

Travel Agency

This is the running example of our paper, as introduced in

Section

1
.
To generate the APIs for both travellers and the agency:

⬇

#

from

directory

-

To run the case study:

⬇

~/
case
-
studies
/
TravelAgency

$

npm

start

Visit
http://localhost:8080
on two web browser windows
side-by-side, one for each traveller.
Execute the Travel Agency service;
you may refer to
https://youtu.be/mZzIBYP_Xac
for an example execution
as a starting point.

(1)

Log in as
Friend
and
Customer
on separate windows.

(2)

As
Friend
, suggest ‘Tokyo’. As
Customer
, query for ‘Tokyo’.
Expect to see that there is no availability.

(3)

As
Friend
, suggest ‘Edinburgh’. As
Customer
, query for ‘Edinburgh’.
Expect to see that there is availability, then ask
Friend
. As
Friend
, enter
a valid numeric split and press
OK
. As
Customer
, enter any string for your name
and any numeric value for credit card and press
OK
. Expect to see that both roles
show success messages.

(4)

Refresh both web browsers and log in as
Friend
and
Customer
on separate windows again.
As
Friend
, suggest ‘Edinburgh’ again. As
Customer
, query for ‘Edinburgh’.
Expect to see that there is no availability, as the last seat has been taken.

Stop the web application by pressing
Ctrl+C
on the terminal.

Data Alignment

1

Observations

Verify the following claims made in the paper against
the tables printed at the end (bottom) of the notebook.

•

Simple Ping Pong (“w/o req”):

–

Time taken by
node
is
less
than time taken by
react
, which entails that
“the round trip time is
dominated by the browser-side message processing time”
.

–

The delta (of
mpst
relative to
bare
) for the
React
endpoints is
greater
than the delta for the
Node
endpoints, which entails that
“
mpst
introduces overhead dominated by the
React.js
session runtime”
.

•

Complex Ping Pong (“w/ req”):

–

Inspect the difference between the message processing time
across
Simple Ping Pong
and
Complex Ping Pong
.
This difference is
greater
for
bare

implementations compared with
mpst

implementations, which entails that

“the UI requirements require
bare
to perform
additional state updates and rendering, reducing the overhead
relative to
mpst
”
.

on the terminal,

.

Definition B.1 (Set of Participants).

{\textnormal{{pt}}\left({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\mu\mathbf{t}.G}}}\right)}&=&{\textnormal{{pt}}\left({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right)}\\

{{p}}}}

The merging operator is defined on local
types. Here, we extend the
merging operator
from
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013

to the extended syntax in
Definition

B.2
.

Definition B.2 (Merging Operator).

The merging operator
\sqcap
on local types
is extended as:

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\oplus\!\langle{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!}\rangle\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!T_{i}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}})}}}\,{\sqcap}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\oplus\!\langle{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!}\rangle\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!T_{i}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}})}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\oplus\!\langle{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!}\rangle\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!T_{i}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\with\!\langle{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!}\rangle\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!T_{i}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}}}}\,{\sqcap}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\with\!\langle{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!}\rangle\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{j}}}\!:\!T^{\prime}_{j}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j\in J}}}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\with\!\langle{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!}\rangle\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{k}}}:T^{\prime\prime}_{k}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{k\in I\cup J}}}}}

\displaystyle\text{where }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime\prime}_{k}}}=\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{k}}}&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{k\in I\setminus J}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{k}}}&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{k\in J\setminus I}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{k}}}}}}\,{\sqcap}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{k}}}}}}&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{k\in I\cap J}}\\
\end{cases}

otherwise, undefined

Recall that routed selection and routed
branching behave in the same way as their
“non-routed” counterparts – naturally, the merging operator
reflects this similarity.

B.1.
Semantics of Local Types

We extend the grammar of local types with

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\looparrowright\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!.~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}:\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!T_{i}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}
,
a construct
to represent that, from the local perspective of
the router, the message
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{j}}}

has been received from
p

but not yet routed
to
q
.
We extend the projection operator to
support this new construct.

\small{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\underset{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!}{\rightsquigarrow}\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!.~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}:\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!G_{i}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}=\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\with\!\langle{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!}\rangle\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\looparrowright\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!.~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}:\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{j}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

LTS Semantics over Local Types

We define the LTS semantics
over local types,
denoted by
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}}}}}
,
in
Fig.

8
.
We highlight and explain the new rules.

[
Lr1
]

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!\oplus\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!T_{i}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[3.5em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}!{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{j}}}}}}}

}\!\!\with\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!T_{i}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[3.5em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}?{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}}}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{j}}}}}

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {

\forall{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}.~\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{i}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{i}}}}}

{{p}}}}

{{q}}}}

\forall{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}.~\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{i}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{i}}}}}

Figure 8.
LTS over Local Types in
RouST

We walk through rules [
Lr4
] and [
Lr5
]
from the perspective of role
p
.

•

[
Lr4
] and [
Lr5
] are
analogue to [
Lr1
] and [
Lr2
]
for sending and receiving messages respectively.
The exception is that the new rules match
on the router role
s
on the local type
and the routed label.

We walk through rules [
Lr6
], [
Lr7
],
[
Lr10
] and [
Lr11
]
from the perspective of role
s
.

•

[
Lr6
] and [
Lr7
] are
analogue to [
Gr1
] and [
Gr2
].
Intuitively, the router
s
holds a
“global” perspective on the interaction
between
p
and
q
, which explains
the correspondence with the LTS semantics over global
types.

•

[
Lr10
] and [
Lr11
] allow
the router to perform routing actions before handling
their own direct communication.
The syntax
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\texttt{via}{\langle\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rangle}({\cdot})}}}

means that the label
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}
is “of the form” of a routing
action, i.e. there exists some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}

such that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\texttt{via}{\langle\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rangle}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}!{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}}})}}}
or

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\texttt{via}{\langle\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rangle}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}?{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}}})}}}
.
The constraint of
\text{subj}({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}})\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}

prevents the violation of the syntactic order of messages
sent and received by
q
.

Curious readers can consider the examples

{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{1}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{2}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
below
to see why this constraint is needed.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{1}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\to\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{r}}}}
}\!\!:{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{M1}}}~}}.~{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{r}}}}
}\!\!-\mspace{-3mu}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!}\!\shortrightarrow\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!:~{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{M2}}}~}}.~{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\texttt{end}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{1}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{r}}}}
}\!\!\oplus{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{M1}}}}}.~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{r}}}}
}\!\!\hookrightarrow\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!:{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{M2}}}~.~{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\texttt{end}}}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{2}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\to\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{r}}}}
}\!\!:{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{M1}}}~}}.~{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!-\mspace{-3mu}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!}\!\shortrightarrow\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{r}}}}
}\!\!:~{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{M2}}}~}}.~{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\texttt{end}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{2}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{r}}}}
}\!\!\oplus{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{M1}}}}}.~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\hookrightarrow\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{r}}}}
}\!\!:{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{M2}}}~.~{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\texttt{end}}}}}}

As for the remaining rules,
[
Lr8
] and [
Lr9
] are the local counterparts to
[
Gr4
] and [
Gr5
] because the router
holds a “global” perspective on the communication,
so transitions that do not violate the syntactic order
of messages between roles
p
and
q

are allowed.

LTS Semantics over Configurations

Let
\mathcal{P}
denote the set of participants in
the communication automaton.
Also let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!}}}
denote the local type of a participant

{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\in\mathcal{P}}
.

A
configuration
describes the
state of the communication automaton with respect to
each participant
{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\in\mathcal{P}}
.
By definition of our LTS semantics, this includes

intermediate
states, so a configuration
would also need to express the state of messages
in transit.

We inherit the definition from
(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
)
,
restated in
Definition

B.3
.

Definition B.3 (Configuration).

A configuration
s=(\vec{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}};~\vec{w})
of a system of
local types
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!}}}\}_{{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\in\mathcal{P}}}

is defined as a pair of:

•

\vec{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!}}})_{{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\in\mathcal{P}}}

is the collection of local types.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!}}}
describes the communication structure
from the local perspective of participant
{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\in\mathcal{P}}
.

•

\vec{w}=(w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}})_{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\neq\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!\in\mathcal{P}}

is the collection of
unbounded buffers
.
The unbounded buffer
w_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}
represents a (FIFO)
queue of messages sent by
p
but not yet
received by
q
.

Remark:

The
subtyping
relation defined on
local types (see
(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
)
)
can be extended to configurations:

\vec{w}=\vec{w}^{\prime}

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}.~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}\prec{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}

(\vec{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}};\vec{w})\prec(\vec{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}}}};\vec{w}^{\prime})

We proceed to define the LTS over configurations in

Definition

B.4
, highlighting the extensions
required for
RouST
.

Definition B.4 (LTS Semantics over Configurations).

The LTS semantics over configurations is defined by
the relation
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{s_{T}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{l}}{s^{\prime}_{T}}}
.

Let
s_{T}=(\vec{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}};~\vec{w})
and
s^{\prime}_{T}=(\vec{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}}}};~\vec{w}^{\prime})
.
We define the specific transitions on
\vec{T}
and
\vec{w}

by case analysis on the label
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}!{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}}}}

Then
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}}~

because
p

initiates the action, so

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}}}}

for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}
.

The message
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}
is in transit from
p
to
q
,
so
w^{\prime}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}=w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}

(
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}
is appended to the queue of in-transit messages
sent from
p
to
q
),
and unrelated buffers
w^{\prime}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}=w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}

are untouched for all
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}?{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}}}}

Then
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}}}~

because
q

initiates the action, so

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}}}}

for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}
.

The message
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}
is no longer in transit
from
p
to
q
as it is received by
q
,
so
w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}\cdot w^{\prime}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}

(
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}
is removed from the front of the queue of in-transit
messages sent from
p
to
q
),
and unrelated buffers
w^{\prime}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}=w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}

are untouched for all
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}={\texttt{via}{\langle\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rangle}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}!{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}}})}

Then
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}}~

because
p

initiates the action.
Because the send action is routed, we also need

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}}
.
This means

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}}

for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}\notin\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}\}
.

The message
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}
is in transit from
p
to
q
,
so
w^{\prime}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}=w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}

and unrelated buffers
w^{\prime}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}=w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}

are untouched for all
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}={\texttt{via}{\langle\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rangle}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}?{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}}})}

Then
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}}}~

because
q

initiates the action.
Because the receive action is routed, we also need

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}}
.
This means

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}}

for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}\notin\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}\}
.

The message
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}
is no longer in transit
from
p
to
q
as it is received by
q
,
so
w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}\cdot w^{\prime}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}
,
and unrelated buffers
w^{\prime}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}=w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}

are untouched for all
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}
.

Routed actions are carried out by the router,
so it makes sense for the local type of the router
to also make a step.
The semantics of the message buffers for routed actions
are the same as their non-routed counterparts; the
only difference is that these message buffers are “managed”
by the router.

Projection for Configurations

When considering the grammar of global types
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}

extended to include intermediate states,
we can obtain the
projected configuration

from a global type
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
with participants
\mathcal{P}
:

{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right\rangle}=\left(\{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}\}_{{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\in\mathcal{P}}}~;~{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right\rangle}_{\{\epsilon\}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}}}\right)

The collection of local types is obtained by
projecting
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
onto each participant
{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\in\mathcal{P}}
.
The contents of the buffers is defined as

{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right\rangle}_{\{w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}\}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}}}
.

\epsilon
denotes an empty buffer.
We inherit the definitions presented in
(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
)
,
and introduce additional rules in

Fig.

9
.

\displaystyle{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\underset{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!}{\rightsquigarrow}\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p'}}}}
}\!\!.~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}:\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!G_{i}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}}}}\right\rangle}_{\{w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}\}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}}}={\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{j}}}}\right\rangle}_{\{w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}\}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}}[w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}}}\mapsto w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}]}

\displaystyle{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!-\mspace{-3mu}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!}\!\shortrightarrow\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p'}}}}
}\!\!:\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!G_{i}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}}}}\right\rangle}_{\{w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}\}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}}}={\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}}\right\rangle}_{\{w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}\}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}}}\text{ for }{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}

\displaystyle\text{since}~\forall{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i,j\in I}}.~{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}}\right\rangle}_{\{w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}\}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}}}={\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{j}}}}\right\rangle}_{\{w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}\}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}}}

Figure 9.
Projection of Buffer Contents from Global Type in

RouST

The semantics of the message buffers
for routed actions are the same as their
non-routed counterparts,
so the projected contents of the buffers
for routed communication are
the same as those under non-routed communication.

Appendix C
Appendix for Proofs in
Section

4

C.1.
Auxiliary Lemmas

Lemma C.1 (Local LTS Preserves Merge).

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{1},T_{2}}}
be local types.
Suppose
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{1}}}}\,{\sqcap}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{2}}}}
exists.

\forall{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{2}}}.~\left((\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{1}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{1}}}}})\wedge(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{2}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{2}}}}})\Longrightarrow({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{1}}}}\,{\sqcap}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{2}}}})\text{ exists}\right)

Proof.

By simultaneous induction on

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{1}}}}\,{\sqcap}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{2}}}}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{1}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{1}}}}}
,
and

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{2}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{2}}}}}
.
∎

Lemma C.2 (Projection and Participation).

\forall{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}.~\left({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\texttt{end}}}\Longleftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}\notin{\textnormal{{pt}}\left({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right)}\right)

Proof.

Prove
(\Longrightarrow)
by induction on the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
.
Prove
(\Longleftarrow)
using the contrapositive (stated below)
by induction on the derivation of
{\textnormal{{pt}}\left({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right)}
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}\in{\textnormal{{pt}}\left({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right)}\Longrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\texttt{end}}}.

∎

Lemma C.3 (Encoding Defines Centroid).

Given an encoding of global type
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
with respect to the
router role
s
,
the router role is the centroid of the encoded communication:

{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\circledast{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
.
∎

Lemma C.4 (Encoding and Substitution Permute).

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G,G^{\prime}}}
be global types,
and
s
be a role.

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G[G^{\prime}/{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\mathbf{t}}}]}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}\left[\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}/{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\mathbf{t}}}\right]

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
.
∎

Lemma C.5 (Encoding Preserves Participants).

\forall{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}.~\left({\textnormal{{pt}}\left({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right)}\subseteq{\textnormal{{pt}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}\right)}\right)

Proof.

The following is logically equivalent:

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}.~\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\in{\textnormal{{pt}}\left({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right)}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\in{\textnormal{{pt}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}\right)}\right)

We prove this by induction on the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
.
∎

Lemma C.6 (Encoding Preserves Privacy).

The encoding on global types will not introduce
non-server roles that were not participants of the
original communication.

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}.~\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\notin{\textnormal{{pt}}\left({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right)}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\notin{\textnormal{{pt}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}\right)}\right)

Proof.

The following is logically equivalent.

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}},G.~\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\in{\textnormal{{pt}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}\right)}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\in{\textnormal{{pt}}\left({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right)}\right)

We prove this by induction on the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
,
assuming that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}
for arbitrary
roles
r
,
s
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.10

The projection of an encoded global type
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
is equal
to the encoded local type after projection
\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{r}}}}
}\!\!,~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket
, with
respect to router
s
, i.e.

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}.~\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}\Longrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}=\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{r}}}}
}\!\!,~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket\right)

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
,

Lemmas

C.5
and
C.6
.
∎

Lemma C.7 (Local Type Encoding Preserves Equality of Projection).

\forall{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}.

\left(({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{1}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}})=({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{2}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}})\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}\Longrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{1}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{2}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}\right)

Proof.

By consequence from
Lemma

4.10
.

Take
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{2}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}
arbitrarily.
Assume
({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{1}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}})=({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{2}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}})
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}
.

We need to show

{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{1}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{2}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
,
but by
Lemma

4.10
, it is sufficient to show

\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{1}}}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{r}}}}
}\!\!,~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket=\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{2}}}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{r}}}}
}\!\!,~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket.

The result follows by congruence from the assumption.

∎

Lemma C.8 (Encoding on Global Types Preserves Merge).

Take global types
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{2}}}

and roles
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}
.
Suppose
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{1}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{2}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
exist.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{1}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}})}}}\,{\sqcap}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{2}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}})}}}\text{ exists}\Longrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{1}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}})}}}\,{\sqcap}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{2}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}})}}}\text{ exists}

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{1}}}}\,{\sqcap}\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{2}}}}
,

Lemmas

C.2
and
C.7
.
∎

Lemma C.9 (Encoding Preserves Projection).

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
be a global type.

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}
.

{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}\text{ exists }\Longrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}\text{ exists }

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
.

(1)

{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\texttt{end}}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\mathbf{t}}}}}

As
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}=G
, if
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
exists,
so does
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
.

(2)

{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\mu\mathbf{t}.G^{\prime}}}}}

By assumption,
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\mu\mathbf{t}.G^{\prime}}}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
exists.
Note that
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
exists regardless of
r
’s
participation in
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}
.

By induction,
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
exists.

To show
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\mu\mathbf{t}.G^{\prime}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
exists,
consider
r
by case:

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\in{\textnormal{{pt}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}\right)}
:

{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\mu\mathbf{t}.G^{\prime}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\mu\mathbf{t}.\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\mu\mathbf{t}.({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}})}}

As
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
exists,
so does
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\mu\mathbf{t}.G^{\prime}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\notin{\textnormal{{pt}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}\right)}
:

{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\mu\mathbf{t}.G^{\prime}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\mu\mathbf{t}.\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\texttt{end}}}

(3)

{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\to\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!:\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!G_{i}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}}}

To determine
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}
, consider
s
by case:

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}\}
:

Then
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\to\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!:\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}
.

To show
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
exists,
consider
r
by case:

–

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}
:
Then
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!\oplus\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}
.

By induction,
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}
exists for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}
.

{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!\oplus\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}
.

As projections of the encoded continuations exist,
so does
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}
.

–

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}
: Follows similarly from above.

–

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\notin\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}\}
:
Then
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}=\underset{i\in I}{{\mbox{\LARGE$\sqcap$}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
,
so the merge exists.

By induction,
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
exists for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}
.

{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}=\underset{i\in I}{{\mbox{\LARGE$\sqcap$}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
,
and this merge exists by
Lemma

C.8
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}\notin\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}\}
:

Then
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!-\mspace{-3mu}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!}\!\shortrightarrow\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!:\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}
.

To show
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
exists,
consider
r
by case:

–

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}
:
Then
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!\oplus\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}
.

By induction,
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}
exists for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}
.

{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!\oplus\!\langle{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!}\rangle\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}
.

As projections of the encoded continuations exist,
so does
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}
.

–

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}
: Follows similarly from above.

–

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}
:
Then
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}=\underset{i\in I}{{\mbox{\LARGE$\sqcap$}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
.

By induction,
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}
exists for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}
.

{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\hookrightarrow\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!:\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}
.

As projections of the encoded continuations exist,
so does
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
.

–

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\notin\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}\}
:
Then
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}=\underset{i\in I}{{\mbox{\LARGE$\sqcap$}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
,
so the merge exists.

By induction,
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
exists for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}
.

{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}=\underset{i\in I}{{\mbox{\LARGE$\sqcap$}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathrm{\llbracket}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}},~\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rrbracket}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}
,
and this merge exists by
Lemma

C.8
.

∎

C.2.
Proof of
Theorem

4.6

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
be a global type with participants

\mathcal{P}={\textnormal{{pt}}\left({{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right)}
,
and let
\vec{T}=\{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}\}_{{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!\in\mathcal{P}}}
be the local
types projected from
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}\approx(\vec{T},\vec{\epsilon})
.

Proof.

Direct consequence of

Lemma

C.10
.
∎

Lemma C.10 (Step Equivalence).

For all global types
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
and configurations
s
,
if
{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right\rangle}\prec s
,
then
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}}\Longleftrightarrow\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{s}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{l}}{s^{\prime}}}

such that
{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle}\prec s^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By induction on the possible transitions in the LTSs
over global types (to prove
\Longrightarrow
,
i.e.
soundness
)
and configurations (to prove
\Longleftarrow
,
i.e.
completeness
).

Notation conventions

We use the following notation for decomposing configurations
and projected configurations.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}s&=&\{T_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}\}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P}},~\{w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}\}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}}\\
s^{\prime}&=&\{T^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}\}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P}},~\{w^{\prime}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}\}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}}\\
{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right\rangle}&=&\{\hat{T_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}\}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P}},~\{\hat{w}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}\}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}}\\
{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle}&=&\{\hat{T^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}\}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P}},~\{\hat{w^{\prime}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}\}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}}\\
\end{array}

Soundness

By rule induction on LTS semantics
over global types.

For each transition
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}}
, we
take the configuration
s={\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right\rangle}
,
derive
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{s}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{l}}{s^{\prime}}}

under the respective LTSs,
and show that
s^{\prime}\prec{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle}
.

The proofs for rules [
Gr1-5
] are
the same as in
(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
)
.
We focus on the new rules introduced for routing.

[

],
where

We have

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\hat{T^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}}}}

\displaystyle\{\hat{w^{\prime}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}\}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}}

Lr6

So
s^{\prime}\prec{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle}
.

[
Gr7
]
where
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {

}\!\!\underset{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {

{{p'}}}}

,

{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{j}}}

Then
s={\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right\rangle}
where

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {

\displaystyle\{w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}\}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}}

\displaystyle={\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{j}}}}\right\rangle}_{\{\vec{\epsilon}\}[w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}}}\mapsto w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p'}}}}}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}]}

Global transition:

We have

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\hat{T^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{j}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}\text{ for }{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}\in\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\{\hat{w^{\prime}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}\}_{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}}

\displaystyle={\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{j}}}}\right\rangle}_{\{\vec{\epsilon}\}}

Configuration transition:

Take
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{r}}}}}}

By [
Lr5
],

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{j}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}

Lr7
],

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{j}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}
.

Also,
{w^{\prime}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}=w_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}}}

Correspondence:

.

s^{\prime}={\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle}
.

[
Gr8
]
where
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {

}\!\!-\mspace{-3mu}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {

}\!\!:\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!G_{i}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{p}}}}
}\!\!-\mspace{-3mu}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {

}\!\!}\!\shortrightarrow\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {

}\!\!:\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l_{i}}}\!:\!G^{\prime}_{i}}\right\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}}}}

By hypothesis,

\forall{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}.~\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}_{i}}}}}

.

By induction,

\forall{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I}}.~\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}}\right\rangle}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{l}}{{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}_{i}}}}\right\rangle}}}
.

To show that
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right\rangle}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{l}}{{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle}}}
,
it is sufficient to show that

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}}}}

for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}=\text{subj}({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}})
,
since the projections for

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q'}}}}\neq\text{subj}({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}})
remain the same.

We know
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}=\underset{i\in I}{{\mbox{\LARGE$\sqcap$}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}

and
{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}=\underset{i\in I}{{\mbox{\LARGE$\sqcap$}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}_{i}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}
.

By induction,

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\underset{i\in I}{{\mbox{\LARGE$\sqcap$}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{i}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\underset{i\in I}{{\mbox{\LARGE$\sqcap$}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}_{i}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}}}}
,
so
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}}}}
.

Gr9

where

}\!\!\underset{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {

, and

{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {

}\!\!\underset{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!}{\rightsquigarrow}\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {

.

By hypothesis,

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{G_{j}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{G^{\prime}_{j}}}}}
,

and
\forall{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i\in I\setminus\{j\}}}.~{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}_{i}=G_{i}}}
.

By induction,

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{j}}}}\right\rangle}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{l}}{{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}_{j}}}}\right\rangle}}}
.

To show that
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right\rangle}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{l}}{{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle}}}

it is sufficient to show that

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}}}}

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G_{j}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}_{j}}}}\!\mathrel{\upharpoonright}\!{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}}}}}}

Completeness

By considering the possible transitions in the LTS
over configurations, defined by
case analysis on the possible labels
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}
.

For each transition
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{s}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{l}}{s^{\prime}}}
,
we take the configuration
s
from the reduction rule,
infer the structure of the global type
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}

such that
s={\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\right\rangle}
,
derive
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{s}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{l}}{s^{\prime}}}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}}

under the respective LTSs,
and show that
s^{\prime}\prec{\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}\right\rangle}
.

The proofs for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}!{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}}

and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}?{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}}

are the same as in
Section

4.2
of
(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
)
.
We focus on the new labels introduced for routing.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}={\texttt{via}{\langle\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{s}}}}
}\!\!\rangle}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{q}}}}!{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}}}})}

Then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {
{{q}}}}
}\!\!\oplus\!\langle{\!\!\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5} {

Also,

We denote this subterm

as subterm.
We denote this subterm
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\tilde{G}}}

Also by definition of projection, no action in

will involve
p
before

Configuration transition:

],

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{T^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{p}}}}}}}}

Lr6
],

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\tilde{T}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}{\tilde{T}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}}}}}

Proof of
Theorem

4.7

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
be a global type.
Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}
is well-formed with respect to some router
s
,
i.e.
\textnormal{{wellFormed}}^{R}\left({\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\textbf{{s}}}}\right)
.

\forall{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}.~\left({\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G}}\to^{*}{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}\Longrightarrow({\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}={\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{\texttt{end}}})\vee\exists{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime\prime}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}.~(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime}}}}\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[1em]{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{l}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.816,0.125,0.565}{G^{\prime\prime}}}}})\right)

Proof.

and
